}

\inputtable{tab_shift_remedy}

\DIFadd{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{tab:shift_remedy} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
gives the sweep at 1,500 null streams and 200 post-change streams per level. The unweighted residual default is valid at $\times 1.0$ and $\times 1.5$, with a false-alarm probability of 0.0020 }\DIFaddend at \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{a realised rate of
\ShiftBreakResidualPfa{} $\in[\ShiftBreakResidualPfaLo,\ShiftBreakResidualPfaHi]$. That is the one shift level this design can certify as broken, not the smallest shift at which it might be.
We report this plainly because it is the
honest boundary of the method: normalising by a fitted $\hat g$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\times 1.5$. It is unresolved at $\times 2.0$ (0.0140, interval }[\DIFadd{0.0092, 0.0213}]\DIFadd{) and invalid at $\times 3.0$ (0.0620, interval }[\DIFadd{0.0509, 0.0754}]\DIFadd{). False alarms cluster immediately after the detector re-enters the calibrated context region, at frames near the support edge where the residual model extrapolates.
}

\DIFadd{Likelihood-ratio weighting holds the level at every shift tested, but its power falls. Its miss rate is 0.095 at $\times 1.0$, against 0.025 unweighted, and rises to 0.540, 0.930 }\DIFaddend and \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\hat\sigma$ absorbs a shift in $P(c)$ only while the model stays adequate, and a large enough move in an unmodelled direction breaks it }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{1.000 at $\times 1.5$}\DIFaddend , \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{but pinning down
exactly how large would need many more replicates than run here. }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\times 2.0$ and $\times 3.0$. The Kish effective-sample-size substitution has no calibration-conditional proof, so the weighted path is a heuristic. The pre-specified rule, reported in Online Resource~1, Table~S8, recommends weighting only if its extra miss rate at $\times 1.0$ is at most 0.05. The extra rate is 0.070, so the unweighted residual stays the default.
}\DIFaddend 

\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{This is where the likelihood-ratio weighting of \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cref{sec:layer1} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
earns its
place, and where our own expectation was wrong. Weighting holds the realised
rate at \ShiftBreakWeightedPfa{} }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{The held-out context gate, which would switch weighting on, is a classifier two-sample test with AUC at least 0.60. It fired on every null stream at every level, including the unshifted $\times 1.0$. A deployment block covers a narrow slice of the diurnal cycle, while calibration spans a day, so a context gate is confounded with benign drift that the residual normalisation already absorbs. Context gating therefore does not separate shifted from unshifted streams.
}

\DIFadd{A stricter support region addresses the boundary failure. The residual calibrator abstains on a frame whose context falls outside a per-coordinate box, or whose Mahalanobis distance exceeds a slack multiple of the calibration 0.999 quantile. The default multiplier is 1.5. We set it to 0.5, so boundary frames abstain. An abstained frame receives a neutral bet, so the supermartingale argument is unchanged. The stricter region holds the level }\DIFaddend at every shift \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{level tested}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{tested. At $\times 3.0$ the false-alarm probability falls from 0.0620 to 0.0007 (interval }[\DIFadd{0.0001, 0.0038}]\DIFadd{), and the abstention rate on null betting instants rises to 0.500. At $\times 1.0$ the miss rate equals the default, 0.025, and abstention is 0.010. Likelihood-ratio weighting is also valid, but its miss rate is 0.540 to 1.000 at $\times 1.5$ to $\times 3.0$, against 0.070 to 0.450 for the stricter region.
}

\DIFadd{The slack value 0.5 was chosen in a 300-stream pilot whose seeds overlap the main run. The pilot tried seven candidate variants: cool-down after an alarm at $k=10$}\DIFaddend , \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{including the ones
where the plain residual construction fails --- but it does so by becoming
drastically conservative, with the miss rate rising from
\ShiftBreakResidualMiss{} to \ShiftBreakWeightedMiss{} at $\times\ShiftResidualBreaks$. It buys validity with power, which is the correct
trade for a guarantee and a poor one for a detector. }%DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdel{The practical recommendation follows directly and is implemented in the library:
run the classifier two-sample test continuously, use the plain residual
construction while it reports no detectable shift }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$25$ and $50$ frames, a box margin halved, slack 1.0, box margin with slack 1.0, and slack 0.5. Of these, only slack 0.5 and cool-down at $k=50$ met the pre-set criterion. We therefore confirmed slack 0.5 on 1,500 null streams and 200 post-change streams per level, with seeds disjoint from both. The evidence covers one shift family, a multiplier on scene activity, in one direction. The false-alarm probabilities are 0.0000, 0.0007}\DIFaddend , \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{0.0007 and 0.0013 at $\times 1.0$, $\times 1.5$, $\times 2.0$ }\DIFaddend and \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{enable weighting once it
does --- accepting that the camera has become a conservative detector and that
the operator should be told the calibration set no longer covers the conditions. An abstention or miss rate that jumps is a signal to recalibrate, not a defect
to hide}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\times 3.0$, and all are valid. The miss rates are 0.010, 0.040, 0.140 and 0.470.
}

\DIFadd{The abstention rate is the operator-visible shift monitor. With slack 0.5 it is 0.010 at $\times 1.0$, 0.068 at $\times 1.5$ and 0.500 at $\times 3.0$. As a suggestion, not a validated rule, recalibrate when the abstention fraction over the monitoring window exceeds 0.05. That threshold separates the unshifted and $\times 1.5$ cases in this sweep, but no other rule or shift was tested. The library default stays at 1.5 because the other experiments were run with it, and changing it would change their results. Where activity shift is expected, slack 0.5 is the suggested setting}\DIFaddend .

\Cref{tab:coverage} varies coverage instead. \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Validity survives all three
regimes, but power does not, and it should not: with calibration from a single
daytime
windowthe }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{With calibration from one daytime
window, the residual construction keeps its level, but its }\DIFaddend miss rate reaches
\CoverDaytimeMiss{} and \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{the abstention rate
\CoverDaytimeAbstain\% . Degradation }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{\CoverDaytimeAbstain\% of frames are abstained on. The
degradation }\DIFaddend appears in the abstention and miss columns, where an operator can see
it, rather than \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{silently }\DIFdelend in the alarm rate\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{, where they
cannot. The operational implication is a requirement rather than a caveat: the
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{. The }\DIFaddend calibration set must \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{therefore }\DIFaddend span the
conditions \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{you intend to monitor}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{to be monitored}\DIFaddend , and the abstention rate \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{tells you }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{shows }\DIFaddend whether it does.

separates the two questions, and they get different answers. }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{The fleet has \NetCameras{} cameras. Each evaluation fleet covers \NetEvalMinutes{}

prior is learned from \NetTrainFleets{} training fleets of $30{,}000$ frames.
Evaluation uses \NetFleets{} fleets with events and \NetFleets{} all-null fleets,

, which has four episodes in 60 minutes. The

.

\DIFdelbegin \paragraph{\DIFdel{The guarantee survives learned control, as claimed.}}
%DIFAUXCMD
\addtocounter{paragraph}{-1}%DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdel{This is the part \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cref{thm:predictable} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
is about, and it holds empirically as
well as formally}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \paragraph{\DIFadd{The guarantee survives learned control.}}
\DIFadd{This is what \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cref{thm:predictable} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
says, and the simulation agrees}\DIFaddend . On all-null
fleets\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{every controller --- }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{, every controller (}\DIFaddend none, hand-designed \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{,
and learned--- produces }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{and learned) gives }\DIFaddend an alarm rate of
\DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{at most }\DIFaddend \NetGnnNullPfa{} and an e-BH false-discovery rate of \NetGnnFdr{}\DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{, }\DIFaddend against
a target of \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\NetFdrLevel}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{\NetFdrLevel{}}\DIFaddend . The learned controller \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{does this while actively }\emph{\DIFdel{hurting}} %DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdel{power, which is the
sharpest available demonstration of the theorem: a badly-behaved learned
component degrades detection and }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{reduces power, and it still
}\DIFaddend leaves the false-alarm guarantee \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{untouched}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{intact}\DIFaddend , because it can \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{only enter }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{act only }\DIFaddend through a
predictable modulation.

\paragraph{The learned spatial prior does not \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{earn its place}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{help}\DIFaddend .}
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{We expected the graph to shorten delay and it does not , on either delaystatistic: conditional }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{The graph does not shorten delay. Conditional }\DIFaddend delay is \NetHeuristicDelaySec\,s \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{(heuristic) }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{for
the hand-designed prior }\DIFaddend and \NetGnnDelaySec\,s \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{(learned ) }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{for the learned one, }\DIFaddend against
\NetNoGraphDelaySec\,s with no graph\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{, and
censored delay --- the comparable statistic per \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cref{sec:problem}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
, since the
three controllers' miss rates differ --- }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{. Censored delay }\DIFaddend is \NetHeuristicCensDelaySec\,s
and \NetGnnCensDelaySec\,s\DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{, }\DIFaddend against \NetNoGraphCensDelaySec\,s. \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{The }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Power is
\NetHeuristicPower{} for the }\DIFaddend hand-designed prior\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{is indistinguishable from no graphat all on either statistic,
with power
\NetHeuristicPower{} against \NetNoGraphPower}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{, \NetNoGraphPower{} with no graph,
and \NetGnnPower{} for the learned controller}\DIFaddend . The learned controller is worse
than \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{both }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{no graph }\DIFaddend on every axis\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{: delay, censored delay, and power \NetGnnPower}\DIFdelend .

\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{We made two attempts to fix this before concluding it, and the second is worth
recording because it failed in an informative direction. The first was
initialisation: the controller's heads are set so that an untrained network
reproduces }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Two changes to the controller did not help. Initialising the heads to reproduce
}\DIFaddend the ungoverned detector \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{exactly, rather than starting at half stake
and a twenty-five-fold hazard, so training begins from the baseline and can only
improve on it in-sample. That }\DIFdelend is now the default\DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{, }\DIFaddend and it is what \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{the table
reports. The second was the objective. Maximising }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cref{tab:network}
}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
reports. Replacing }\DIFaddend terminal log-wealth \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{is
plainly the wrong target --- detection delay is the time the wealth takes to
reach $1/\alpha$, so a camera that ends the stream rich but climbed slowly is no
use --- and we replaced it }\DIFdelend with log-wealth credited over a \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{window of
}\DIFaddend \NetCreditWindowSec\,s \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{window opening at }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{from }\DIFaddend each camera's \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{own onset , trained and
evaluated exactly as the terminal-objective controller was. The
better-motivated objective performed }\emph{\DIFdel{worse}}%DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdel{: power fell }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{onset made power fall }\DIFaddend from
\NetGnnPower{} to \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\NetGnnWindowedPower. We report the stronger of the two
configurations in \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cref{tab:network} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
and include both there, and
keep }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{\NetGnnWindowedPower{}. Both configurations are reported, and
}\DIFaddend the windowed objective \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{stays }\DIFaddend in the library behind a flag\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{so the comparison reproduces. }%DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdel{That a better-motivated objective makes things worse, and that the training gain
under it drifts downward rather than up, both point the same way: the gradient
signal is weak, not the optimiser mistuned. There is little for the controller to learn.
}%DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdel{Our reading is that the spatial signal in these streams is real but small
relative to what a camera learns fromits own p-values: an episode propagates to
a neighbour with probability $0.45$ after a lag of order a thousand frames, by
which time the neighbour's own evidence usually dominates}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{. The controller has
little signal to learn from}\DIFaddend . A denser fleet, stronger propagation \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{, or a site layout where a single access road feeds many
cameras might change that, and we have not testedthose. What we can say is that
on the configuration reported here the graph layer's value lies entirely in
e-BH, not in the spatial prior}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{or a shared
access road might change this, but these were not tested}\DIFaddend .

\paragraph{Fleet-level FDR control does earn its place.}
e-BH recovers the affected cameras at power \NetNoGraphPower{}\DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{, }\DIFaddend with a realised
false-discovery rate of \NetNoGraphFdr{} against a target of \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\NetFdrLevel,
under dependence induced by }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{\NetFdrLevel{}. The
dependence comes from }\DIFaddend shared weather, shared lighting and correlated episodes.
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{That }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{This part of the fleet layer }\DIFaddend is the part \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{of Layer 3 we would keep}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{worth keeping}\DIFaddend .

\subsection{Computational cost}
\label{sec:compute}

\DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \inputtable{tab7_compute}
%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \inputtable{tab_cost_breakdown}
\DIFaddend 

\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{tab:compute} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
measures what an added-cost claim can be measured to mean:
a same-CPU ratio against a stand-in backbone we built to the scale of a real
edge detector, not a measurement on a specific published model or deployed
hardware,
and one that excludes the cost of extracting the low-dimensional
context features Layer 1 consumes (time of day, a weather flag, a
background-subtraction motion measure) and the peak memory the guarantee layer needs, neither of which this table reports. Within that scope: the
reference
backbone --- a depthwise-separable stack with \CostBackboneParams}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{The conformal p-value and the e-detector step run only at betting instants,
one per decorrelation lag. With $L=\CostLag{}$, the batch path costs
$(0.34+13.69)/20 = 0.702$}\DIFaddend \,\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{M
parameters at $320\times320$, timed on the same CPU --- costs
\CostBackboneMs}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \textmu \DIFadd{s per frame predicted, with the measured cost
given below. The streaming path, which a deployed camera runs, costs
$(0.34+39.28)/20 = 1.98$}\DIFaddend \,\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{ms }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \textmu \DIFadd{s per frame predicted. The guarantee layer therefore
costs under two microseconds per frame on either path. The lag $L=20$ is the
decorrelation lag estimated on the 200}{\DIFadd{,}}\DIFadd{000-frame benchmark stream. The simulation
experiments use a lag of 18, and the lags selected on real video range from 1 to
30. Because the batch cost scales as $1/L$, a lag of 1 gives about 14\,}\textmu \DIFadd{s }\DIFaddend per
frame.
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{SENTINEL-E adds \CostSentinelUs}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin 

\DIFadd{The measured end-to-end cost is $0.610$}\DIFaddend \,\textmu s per frame\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{amortised, or \CostSentinelPct\% of the backbone, running at
\CostRealtimeFactor$\times$ real time on one thread. Fitting a camera's
calibration takes \CostFitSec}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{, or 0.122}\DIFaddend \,\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{s once. The reason the added cost is this small
is structural rather than incidental: the per-frame arithmetic is the $O(1)$
forward recursion of \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cref{eq:forward}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
, and it runs once per decorrelation lag
rather than once per frame. Hardware that already runs the detector in real time, and already extracts the context features, needs no additional
budget}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{s over a
200,000-frame stream. It includes the $O(T)$ expansion of the bets onto the
frame grid, which costs 0.017\,}\textmu \DIFadd{s per frame. The prediction is about 15\%
above the measurement, because the per-call component timings carry overhead
that one long run amortises. Against the 36.84\,ms frozen backbone, the measured
cost is 0.0017\% per frame, and the predicted cost is 0.0019\%.
}

\DIFadd{The figures exclude context-feature extraction, video decoding, the backbone
itself and peak memory. The backbone is a stand-in for an edge detector, with
\CostBackboneParams{}\,M parameters at $320\times320$, not a published model.
Fitting a camera's calibration takes \CostFitSec{}\,s, once}\DIFaddend .

\subsection{Checking the assumptions}
\label{sec:diagnostics}

\inputtable{tab9_diagnostics}
\inputtable{tab10_pcoverage}

The guarantee rests on two premises\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{that are assumptions rather than theorems}\DIFdelend . The first \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{, conditional independence, is checkable from the calibration set
alone, so an operator can run it on their own camera before trusting the
alarm
rate ; the second needs labelled null deployment data an operator does not
normally have, and we report it here as a check on the
library rather than a
field-deployable one. \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{tab:diagnostics} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
reports conditional independence:
betting }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{is conditional super-uniformity of
the betting increments. The second is marginal calibration of the p-values, which is checked in simulation
below. The first is testable, and the conditional-mean test on real video
contradicts it (\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cref{sec:real-data}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
). The operational consequence of both is the
per-video false-alarm rate measured on real video in \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cref{sec:real-data}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
.
}

\DIFadd{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{tab:diagnostics} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
shows the conditional-independence diagnostics on the
calibration residuals of \DiagStreams{} simulated cameras. Betting }\DIFaddend on every frame leaves a
lag-one \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{residual }\DIFdelend autocorrelation of \DiagAcfRaw{} and a \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{median }\DIFaddend Ljung--Box \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{p-value of
\DiagLjungRaw, which rejects the
premise outright, while betting }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$p$ of
\DiagLjungRaw. Betting }\DIFaddend once per estimated lag\DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{, at a mean lag of \DiagLagMean{}
frames, }\DIFaddend gives \DiagAcfThin{} and \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\DiagLjungThin{} --- markedly better, but \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cref{tab:diagnostics}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
's own
pass-rate row shows this }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{a median $p$ of \DiagLjungThin{}. Thinning }\DIFaddend reduces
the violation \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{rather than removing it: see
\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cref{sec:limitations}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
. \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{tab:pcoverage} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
checks Layer 1 in isolation on
$191{,}890$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{but does not remove it. Only \DiagLjungFrac{} of cameras pass the
Ljung--Box test at 5\%. The diagnostic therefore rejects conditional independence after
thinning. What the experiments support is the empirical false-alarm control of
\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cref{sec:validity} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
and \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cref{sec:real-data}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
, not the literal hypothesis of
\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cref{thm:episodic}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
.
}

\DIFadd{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{tab:pcoverage} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
checks the marginal calibration of the deployment p-values.
Over \DiagPN{} }\DIFaddend betting instants pooled from labelled null streams\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{: the deployment
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{, the }\DIFaddend p-values
are super-uniform at every level tested\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{, with a smallest attainable
value of \DiagPmin{}--- the tail resolution that \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cref{thm:beta} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
buys and that
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{. The smallest observed p-value is
\DiagPmin{}. At a calibration size of \CalN{}, the exact Beta level is
\BetaPmin{}, against \DkwPmin{} for }\DIFaddend the DKW inflation\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{would forfeit --- but this is a validation of
the library
against ground truth we constructed, not a check }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{, a factor of
\BetaOverDkwSpeedup{}. The check uses labelled null streams, which an operator
does not have. The thinned calibration set is the quantity }\DIFaddend an operator can \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{run on an
unlabelled camera}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{compute}\DIFaddend .

.

rather than an independent

the exact Beta levels. Both rows are valid, so the gap is a difference in power

assumes i.i.d.\ calibration draws, and thinning by the

\DIFadd{The invalid rows are as follows. Betting on every frame gives PFA $1.0000$
}[\DIFadd{0.9974, 1.0000}]\DIFadd{, so every null stream alarms, with a mean time to false alarm
of 337 frames. Pooled conformal calibration gives PFA $0.9887$ }[\DIFadd{0.9819, 0.9929}]\DIFadd{.
Omitting the calibration-conditional correction ($\delta=0$) gives PFA
$0.0387$ }[\DIFadd{0.0300, 0.0497}]\DIFadd{, nearly four times nominal. Without the correction the
guarantee fails, and pooled calibration is not a valid substitute.
}

\DIFadd{The other twelve variants of the 200-stream table are descriptive. They are
listed in Online Resource~1, Table~S7. At 200 streams, 14 of the 19 variants are
unresolved, so that table gives point estimates, not verdicts. Two of the twelve
resolve as invalid at that count: bet once per five frames (PFA 0.935) and
Mondrian conformal (0.025 }[\DIFadd{0.011, 0.057}]\DIFadd{). The }\DIFaddend fixed-start \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{product martingale (}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{variant, which has }\DIFaddend no
changepoint prior\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{) misses \AblPointPriorMiss{} of events: with the change arriving deep into the stream, a }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{, misses 1.00 of events. A }\DIFaddend wealth process started at frame one
has \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{burned too much to recover, which is precisely the
failure mode the }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{spent too much when a late change arrives. The }\DIFaddend mixture over onsets in
\cref{eq:episodic} \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{exists to prevent,
and the structural reason SENTINEL-E outperforms E-SHIFT here: E-SHIFT's
wealth also starts at frame one}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{prevents that failure}\DIFaddend .

\section{\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Limitations}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Discussion and limitations}\DIFaddend }
\label{sec:limitations}

\paragraph{\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Real video exposes a }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Power on }\DIFaddend real \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{power gap this paper does not close, and
it is not illegal-dumping footage}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{video depends on the backbone}\DIFaddend .}
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{This is the main limitation and we do not
want it buried. \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{sec:real-data} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
runs the full pipeline on real, continuous surveillance video with a real backbone, and it is }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Optical flow detects 11 of 12 Ped2 episodes, but 3 of 74 Avenue episodes and none of the 50 Mivia-IWDD dumping videos. PatchCore detects 7, 9 and 10 respectively (\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cref{tab:r3_test}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
). PatchCore is a strong training-free appearance backbone, but it is not the state of the art on these benchmarks (\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cref{sec:real-data}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
). The power figures therefore bound what such frozen backbones deliver, not what the best published detectors would deliver. On Mivia-IWDD, PatchCore detects 5 of 25 static and 5 of 25 dynamic dumping events, and flow detects none (Online Resource~1, Table~S3). The labels give onset only, so this split cannot show whether the detector responds to the action or to the waste left behind. A persistent residue is }\DIFaddend the \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{paper's most
important negative-adjacent result: on CUHK Avenue, real autocorrelation and
real episode brevity leave a valid but weak detection window
(\RealAvenuePfaHeadline{} realised }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{permanent-change special case of the episodic mixture, but no run here tests an appearance backbone on residue.
}

\paragraph{\DIFadd{False alarms on real video are consistent with the nominal level, but the evidence is thin.}}
\DIFadd{On the single-camera corpora the }\DIFaddend false-alarm \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{rate at $\alpha=0.1$, but a
\RealAvenueMissHeadline{} miss rate); Ped2 fares better but is still far from a
comfortable margin. That gap did not exist in the simulated streams, and no
simulator we could have built would have exposed it, because it comes from a
specific, measured property of real optical-flow scores --- a decorrelation
lag (\RealAvenueFullLag{} frames on Avenue, \RealPedTwoFullLag{} on Ped2)
close to or exceeding a real episode's own duration --- that an AR(}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{counts are }\DIFaddend 1 \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{)
process with a short memory
cannot reproduce by construction. What \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cref{sec:real-data} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
establishes is that the guarantee holds on real video at the lag its own diagnostic selects, on two different real camera scenes, and that every classical baseline tested
fails to hold it on the same real streams. What it does not establish is that
this backbone, at this frame rate, is adequate for deployment, or that the
result transfers to illegal dumping specifically: Avenue and }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{of 3 and 0 of 3 for Ped2, and 0 of 16 and 1 of 16 for Avenue. The null footage is 12.6\,s of }\DIFaddend Ped2 \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{are
classical pedestrian-walkway anomaly corpora, not dumping footage, because no
released corpus --- }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{and 6.9\,min of Avenue, too little to resolve per-video rates. Ped2 flow at $\alpha=0.2$ has 3 of 3 false alarms (Online Resource~1, Table~S1). On }\DIFaddend Mivia-IWDD \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{included --- provides months of continuously-labelled per-frame dumping-detector output, and the two are not
the same task (dumping is committed once and its evidence often persists in
the scene; a pedestrian or vehicle anomaly is transient by construction, which
is part of why Avenue's episodes are so short).
The library ships the
splicing protocol and dataset adapters needed to run the identical evaluation
on any other corpus with real per-frame scores}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{at $\alpha=0.1$, PatchCore raises alarms on 9 of 50 normal videos, with a 95\% interval of }[\DIFadd{0.086, 0.314}] \DIFadd{that contains the nominal level, and flow raises them on 4 of 50. Per hour of null footage the rates are far higher than the per-video figures suggest, because the detector restarts after each alarm and alarms cluster within a video (\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cref{sec:real-data}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
).
}

\paragraph{\DIFadd{Candidate causes of the Mivia-IWDD excess.}}
\DIFadd{The point estimate exceeds the nominal level}\DIFaddend , and the \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{adapters raise an
error rather than substituting synthetic data when those scores are absent;
what would close the gap \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cref{sec:real-data} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
exposes is not a different
evaluation but a backbone whose frame-to-frame errors are more nearly
independent than optical-flow magnitude's, which is future work. }%DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\paragraph{\DIFdel{The tested horizon is two orders of magnitude short of the
motivating claim.}}
%DIFAUXCMD
\addtocounter{paragraph}{-1}%DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdel{The abstract and introduction motivate the problem with a camera running
``continuously for weeks'' between recalibrations. The longest horizon actually
swept in \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cref{tab:horizon} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
is \HorizonMaxHours{} hours; a week is $168$ hours,
roughly $60\times$ longer, and a month roughly $260\times$ longer. Nothing in
\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cref{thm:episodic} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
or \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cref{thm:beta} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
depends on the horizon --- the time-uniform guarantee holds at $10^6$ frames exactly as it does at $10^4$, and that is }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{data do not say why. Three candidates fit the design. Pooled calibration across cameras is }\DIFaddend the \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{entire content of the word ``time-uniform'' --- so the theoretical
claim is unaffected. What is untested is whether the }\emph{\DIFdel{simulator}}%DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdel{'s
non-stationarity assumptions, and in particular the diurnal and weather
processes that drive the shift studied in \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cref{sec:shift}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
, remain plausible
representations of real footage at a week's scale rather than a few hours';
slow drifts that do not appear inside \HorizonMaxHours{} hours could plausibly
compound over a week in ways this experiment cannot show either way. Extending
\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cref{tab:horizon}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
's sweep by two orders of magnitude is mechanical (the
simulator scales to any $T$) and is one of the two directions we would
prioritise in revision}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{first. The guarantee is per camera, and every test camera is unseen, so the per-camera premise is not tested. Serial dependence of the p-values is the second (next paragraph). Boundary selection of the lag is the third. $L^\ast=7$ is the smallest lag whose leave-one-video-out proportion is at most $\alpha$, and it sits exactly on the boundary at 20 of 200 $=0.100$. Fifty null videos cannot separate these effects, and this paper names none of them as the cause.
}

\paragraph{\DIFadd{Real-video baselines show no demonstrated advantage.}}
\DIFadd{On the real-video comparisons in \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cref{sec:real-data}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
, SENTINEL-E shows no advantage over the baselines. On Mivia-IWDD, PatchCore CUSUM has the same per-video false-alarm count (9 of 50), detects more episodes (11 against 10) and has a lower rate per hour (53.6 against 100.5). Fifteen of the 36 baseline configurations at $\alpha=0.1$ had no admissible knob and used the most conservative setting, so those baselines are not tuned. The advantage claimed for SENTINEL-E is the horizon-free guarantee, and the short real videos cannot exercise it}\DIFaddend .

\paragraph{Conditional super-uniformity \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{is an assumption, }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{does }\DIFaddend not \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{a theorem}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{hold on real video}\DIFaddend .}
\Cref{thm:episodic} \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{needs the }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{requires the betting }\DIFaddend p-values to be super-uniform given the past. Thinning \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{by the decorrelation lag makes the residual dependence small ---
\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cref{tab:diagnostics} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
reports the }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{does not deliver this on real footage, so the hypothesis fails there. The thinned p-values remain positively correlated (lag-one correlation between \RThreeDepCorrMin{} and \RThreeDepCorrMax{} at the selected lags), and after a p-value below $0.1$ the next has mean \RThreeDepCondMin{} to \RThreeDepCondMax{}, far below the $0.5$ that conditional super-uniformity implies (\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cref{tab:r3_dependence}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
). The }\DIFaddend Ljung--Box \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{check \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citep{ljung1978} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
--- but it
does not eliminate it, and no finite-sample test can certify its absence.
\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{thm:beta} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
additionally assumes i.i.d.\ calibration draws, which is why the
calibration set is thinned too.
}%DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\paragraph{\DIFdel{The residual model can be wrong.}}
%DIFAUXCMD
\addtocounter{paragraph}{-1}%DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdel{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{eq:residual} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
assumes the normalised residual is context-free. Where it is
not, calibration is approximate; the exactly group-conditional Mondrian variant
is available for the cases where a single context variable dominates, and the
support check bounds the damage from extrapolation but does not detect
misspecification inside the support.
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{test also rejects independence for most simulated cameras \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citep{ljung1978}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
. The marginal distribution shows no excess of small values, and the per-video false-alarm rate is consistent with $0.1$ on Ped2 and Avenue, where the null footage is short. Neither repairs the formal argument. On real video the time-uniform bound is therefore an empirical property of the selected operating point, not a theorem. Betting on blocks rather than frames, or e-processes built for dependent data, would be needed to restore it formally.
}\DIFaddend 

\DIFdelbegin \paragraph{\DIFdel{Calibration robustness has one measured breaking point, and several
unresolved shift levels below it.}} %DIFAUXCMD
\addtocounter{paragraph}{-1}%DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdel{The residual construction is clearly
invalid at $\times\ShiftResidualBreaks$ shift in an unmodelled covariate
(\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cref{tab:activity}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
): the Wilson interval's lower end clears nominal there. At \ShiftResidualUnresolvedRatios{} shift the realised rate is not
distinguishable from nominal at the replication count run here, which is not
the same claim as validity --- only \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cref{sec:validity}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
's much larger
replicate count resolves that question, and it was not re-run at every shift
level. Likelihood-ratio weighting holds a realised rate of \ShiftBreakWeightedPfa{}
at the one level we can certify as broken for the unweighted construction, but
costs most of the power there (miss rate \ShiftBreakWeightedMiss{} against
\ShiftBreakResidualMiss{}); we did not verify it holds at the unresolved
levels below that, and \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cref{sec:layer1} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
discloses that its calibration
substitutes an approximation (the Kish effective sample size) for the exact
order-statistic argument, which is not itself derived to be valid under
likelihood-ratio weights.
So the honest summary is that }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \paragraph{\DIFadd{Long-horizon evidence is simulated.}}
\DIFadd{The real videos last seconds to minutes, so the week-long result rests on simulation. \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{tab:horizon_week} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
runs 200 streams per horizon from 1\,h to 168\,h, with a simulated 24\,h diurnal cycle at 25\,fps. }\DIFaddend SENTINEL-E \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{'s
false-alarm guarantee is robust to }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{raised no false alarm at any horizon. Its 95\% upper limit is 0.019, which does not by itself resolve $\alpha=0.01$. The fixed threshold reaches 0.980 within an hour. The simulator's assumptions, namely the diurnal and weather processes and Student-$t$ AR(1) noise, are untested against weeks of real footage. A slow drift invisible within a day could compound over a week, and no experiment here shows either way. The time-uniform guarantee does not depend on the horizon, so this is a question about the simulator, not }\DIFaddend the \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{shift a well-specified residual model
can absorb and to anything the two-sample test catches, and is }\emph{\DIFdel{not}}
%DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdel{unconditionally robust: a large shift in a direction the model does not
capture, arriving faster than the shift test can react, will inflate the rate,
and exactly how large is not established by this experiment beyond the one
level that clearly breaks. We know of no distribution-free method for which
robustness is unconditional, but that does not make it a non-issue, and an operator should treat a rising abstention or miss rate as a prompt to
recalibrate. }%DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\paragraph{\DIFdel{The episode model is a model.}}
%DIFAUXCMD
\addtocounter{paragraph}{-1}%DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdel{The two-state chain of \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cref{eq:episodic} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
assumes episodes with geometric
durations and geometric gaps, and a constant within-episode anomalous fraction.
Real dumping episodes are none of those exactly. The prior grid spans a wide
range of each, and \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cref{thm:episodic} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
holds whatever the truth is --- the
guarantee never depends on the episode model being correct, only the power does .
But we have not characterised how power degrades when the true duration
distribution is far from anything on the grid, and a heavy-tailed duration
distribution is the case we would expect to be worst. The generalisation also
costs a little where it is not needed: with a single persistent change the episodic mixture is marginally behind the changepoint mixture
(\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cref{tab:episodes}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
), because the prior spends mass on episode-end hypotheses
that never fire}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{theorem. The shorter sweep, up to \HorizonMaxHours\,h, is in Online Resource~1, Table~S6}\DIFaddend .

\paragraph{\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Every headline comparison runs at one simulator configuration}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Covariate shift is the main threat to calibration}\DIFaddend .}
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{tab:delay_far,tab:ablation,tab:episodes} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
all use $800$-frame episodes at $35\%$ intermittency with a $6{,}000$-frame mean gap; \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cref{sec:episodes}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
's own
sweep over episode count and intermittency (\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cref{tab:episodes,tab:intermittency}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
)shows the episodic mixture's advantage is not uniform across that grid --- it
is absent or reversed at one episode and at low intermittency
(\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cref{sec:dilation}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
) --- so a reader should not extrapolate the headline
numbers to a configuration outside the swept range. We have not run the full
baseline comparison or the ablation at multiple points in $(\text{episode length}, \text{gap}, \pi)$-space, only at the two axes
\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cref{sec:episodes} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
varies one at a time; a joint sweep is mechanical but was
not run here.
}%DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\paragraph{\DIFdel{Fleet scale and graph knowledge.}}
%DIFAUXCMD
\addtocounter{paragraph}{-1}%DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdel{Experiments use \NetCameras{} cameras with a known planar geometry. Larger fleets
are computationally trivial (\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cref{tab:compute}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
), but we have not tested whether
the learned prior transfers across topologies, and a municipality whose camera
positions are poorly recorded would need to infer the graph. }%DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\paragraph{\DIFdel{An operator cannot, from the alarm stream alone, tell a working
camera from a silently conservative one.}}
%DIFAUXCMD
\addtocounter{paragraph}{-1}%DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdel{A camera whose calibration set has drifted out of support abstains and bets
neutrally (\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cref{sec:layer1}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
); one whose realised miss rate has risen because
of }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{The unweighted residual default holds its level at $\times 1.0$ and $\times 1.5$, but at $\times 3.0$ its false-alarm probability is 0.0620 (\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cref{tab:shift_remedy}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
). A stricter support region, with slack 0.5, holds the level at every shift tested, at the cost of abstention. Its selection is disclosed in \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cref{sec:shift}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
. Seven pilot variants were tried, only two met the pre-set criterion, and the evidence covers one activity-shift family in one direction. Likelihood-ratio weighting also holds the level, but its miss rate reaches 1.000 at $\times 3.0$, and its effective-sample-size substitution has no proof. The context gate fired on every null stream, so it is not a usable monitor. Operators should watch the abstention rate. As a suggestion, not a validated rule, recalibrate when the abstention fraction exceeds 0.05. A miss rate that rises under }\DIFaddend an unmodelled shift produces \DIFdelbegin \emph{\DIFdel{fewer}} %DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{fewer }\DIFaddend alarms, not more, \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{and looks
identical from outside to a camera at a genuinely quiet site.
The miss rate
itself is unobservable in the field, by definition --- it is a count over
events that were never flagged --- and the one instrument that could catch a
silent failure, the two-sample shift test of \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cref{sec:layer1}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
, is off unless
likelihood-ratio weighting is explicitly enabled, which is not the default
path reported in \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cref{tab:validity,tab:delay_far}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
. We do not have a fix for
this in the present work; a deployed system should expose the abstention rate,the shift-test statistic, and the calibration set's age as first-class
operator-facing signals rather than internal diagnostics, and should not
default the shift test off. }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{so the alarm stream alone does not reveal it.
}\DIFaddend 

\DIFdelbegin \paragraph{\DIFdel{Deployment guidance this paper does not give.}}
%DIFAUXCMD
\addtocounter{paragraph}{-1}%DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdel{Nothing here tells an operator how to choose $\alpha$, how large a calibration
set to collect before trusting the alarm rate, or when a rising abstention or
miss rate should trigger recalibration rather than a shrug. \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{sec:validity}
}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
fixes $\alpha$ andstudies its consequences; it does not study how a
municipality should set it against the cost of a false dispatch and }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \paragraph{\DIFadd{The learned spatial prior does not improve delay.}}
\DIFadd{The learned prior keeps the false-alarm guarantee, as \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cref{thm:predictable} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
requires, but it does not shorten delay (\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cref{sec:network}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
). Its transfer to other camera topologies is untested.
}

\paragraph{\DIFadd{The episodic advantage is configuration-specific.}}
\DIFadd{In the corner sweep (\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cref{tab:corner_sweep}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
), episodes help when they are long, intermittent and recurring. With 1600-frame episodes, $\pi=0.2$ and a 12}{\DIFadd{,}}\DIFadd{000-frame gap, the episodic mixture is ahead by 141.1\,s (95\% CI +79.6 to +210.2\,s). A single 800-frame episode favours the changepoint mixture by 55.7\,s (95\% CI $-111.2$ to $-14.0$). Dense and permanent changes are equivalent under the 1\,s margin of \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cref{sec:episodes}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
, and 200-frame episodes are missed by both mixtures. The delay comparison and the ablation use one configuration, four episodes in 60 minutes (\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cref{sec:delay}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
). Unpaired comparisons on different grids are descriptive (Online Resource~1, Tables~S4 and~S5). The guarantee does not depend on the episode model, but power does, and heavy-tailed episode durations, which dumping may produce, are untested.
}

\paragraph{\DIFadd{Deployment needs more than a false-alarm rate.}}
\DIFadd{The time-uniform guarantee is the horizon-free analogue of }\DIFaddend the \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{cost
of a missed event, which is a decision-theoretic question this paper leaves
open. Relevant literatures we have not engaged include alarm management and
human factors (how many alerts per shift a human operator can act on before
alarm fatigue sets in), classical signal detection theory (the ROC-operating-point
framing a control-room already uses }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{in-control run length $\ARL$. Control charts also need a recalibration cadence, which statistical process control treats as a design choice \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citep{montgomery2020introduction}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
, and this paper does not set one. A ROC operating point \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citep{fawcett2006introduction} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
describes a per-decision trade-off that control rooms already use }\DIFaddend for other sensors\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{), statistical process
control practice (where a control chart's recalibration cadence is itself a studied design choice), and public-sector algorithmic accountability (what
disclosure a municipality owes residents about an automated flagging system
run against public spaces). Each bears directly on how this method would
actually be operated, and none of it is in \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cref{sec:related}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
.
}%DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\paragraph{\DIFdel{Governance and human oversight.}}
%DIFAUXCMD
\addtocounter{paragraph}{-1}%DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdel{This is a detector, not a decision-maker, and the gap between those two is where the harder questions live. An alarm is a prompt for a human to look at
footage and decide whether dumping occurred and who is responsible; the false-alarm
guarantee in this paper says nothing about what happens after that prompt ---
whether the human reviewing it is adequately resourced, whether the review is
itself audited, or whether the system is used to justify automated
enforcement actionwithout one. Camera placement, retention policy, and who
may query the alarm history are governance decisions this paper does not make
and should not be read as endorsing any particular answerto. A municipality
deploying this or a similar system should treat the statistical guarantee as
one input to a decision that also needs an explicit data-retention }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{. By contrast, $\alpha$ bounds false alarms over a whole monitoring period, so an alarm budget per period is the operational counterpart. Hourly alarm rates on real video are large (\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cref{sec:real-data}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
), and a deployment should budget them as well as per-video rates. Choosing $\alpha$ against the costs of a false dispatch and a missed event is left open. Every alarm should go to a named human reviewer before any action. Public-space surveillance also raises accountability questions that the guarantee does not answer. A deployment needs a retention }\DIFaddend policy, a named \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{human reviewer in the loop }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{reviewer }\DIFaddend before any enforcement \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{action}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{step}\DIFaddend , and a \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{mechanism by which a resident }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{route by which residents }\DIFaddend can contest a flagged event\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{--- none of which
follows from, or is addressed by, the anytime-valid construction itself}\DIFdelend .

\section{Conclusion}
\label{sec:conclusion}

\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Waste-surveillance systems are evaluated per clip and deployed per month, and
the statistical consequence of that mismatch is larger than the field's
reporting practice reveals: valid }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Clip-level evaluation hides a statistical problem. A }\DIFaddend per-frame \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{rule fed valid }\DIFaddend p-values \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{, applied with the obvious
stopping rule , produce }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{raises }\DIFaddend a false alarm on \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{essentially every multi-hour null stream
we simulated. The 2026 IWDD contest, drawing ten teams, reports per-clip $F_1$
plus a notification-delay measure and, in its own overview, a per-clip ROC
analysis; none of that reports a rate over a continuous monitoring horizon}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{\ValidityPfaPvaluePct\% of 40-minute null streams. This paper makes three contributions}\DIFaddend .

\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Closing }\emph{\DIFdel{that}} %DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdel{gap does not need the episodic construction: any
finite-sample anytime-valid e-process --- including the changepoint mixture
this paper takes as its own point of departure, which by
\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cref{prop:generalisation} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
is a faithful instance of prior work
\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citep{saha2026nonpartitioned} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
--- composed with the same conformal front end
already holds the nominal level in our own experiments (\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cref{sec:ablation}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
). What required the new construction is a narrower, and still real, claim: a
dumping event is an episode that ends and recurs, and mixing over episodes rather than changepoint locations buys back detection power in that regime
without touching the guarantee. The episodic }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{The theory is an episodic }\DIFaddend e-process\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{mixes over when an
episode starts }\emph{\DIFdel{and stops}}%DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdel{, remains an exact supermartingale }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{. It mixes over episodes that start, end and recur. It is a non-negative supermartingale when the p-values are conditionally super-uniform, }\DIFaddend so Ville's inequality \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{applies unchanged, costs $O(1)$ per frame, and }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{gives time-uniform false-alarm control. It }\DIFaddend contains the changepoint mixture \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{exactly as the boundary case $\eta = 0$. Around it,
conformal calibration conditional on observable context makes the p-values
honest, and e-BH turns per-camera evidence into fleet-wide decisions under
arbitrary dependence. Three components did not survive contact with measurement and are reported rather than removed: the dilated emission for
within-episode intermittency, which the betting mixture already handles; the
learned spatial prior, which leaves the guarantee exactly intact --- as its
theorem promises --- while failing to improve detection; and the size, and
even the sign, of }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{as }\DIFaddend the \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{episodic mixture's power advantage over the changepoint
mixture, which \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cref{sec:episodes} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
resolves with a paired analysis on matched
seeds and a shared betting grid rather than the unpaired comparison an
earlier, confounded version of that experiment reported.
Both mixtures are , in our comparison, among the methods that hold their
nominal false-alarm level; SENTINEL-E is }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{case in which episodes never end. Real video does not meet the conditional premise (\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cref{sec:limitations}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
), so the guarantee there is an empirical property, not a theorem.
}

\DIFadd{The algorithm is a two-state forward recursion. It runs in constant time per frame, with }\DIFaddend the \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{only one of the two that keeps
the episode-recurrence axis available at the same $O(1)$ cost, at
\CostSentinelPct\% of the cost of the backbone it wraps --- with a measured
boundary, reported rather than elided, beyond which a shift in an unmodelled
covariate breaks it unless the conservative safeguard is engaged. }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{p-value and the e-detector step computed once per betting lag. The guarantee layer costs \CostSentinelUs\,}\textmu \DIFadd{s per frame in batch and about 2\,}\textmu \DIFadd{s per frame when streaming (1.98\,}\textmu \DIFadd{s predicted).
}\DIFaddend 

\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{This revision closes part of the first empirical direction the earlier version
of this paper deferred: \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cref{sec:real-data} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
runs the identical pipeline,
unmodified, on real }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{The evaluation reports realised rather than nominal false-alarm rates, censored delays and paired comparisons on grids of equal size.
}

\DIFadd{The simulated results are the strongest. No false alarm occurred in 200 week-long null streams (95\% upper limit 0.019, which does not by itself resolve $\alpha=0.01$), while }\DIFaddend per-frame \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{scores from two real surveillance corpora,
CUHK Avenue and UCSD }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{thresholds fail within an hour. The episodic mixture shortens delay for long, intermittent and recurring events. For a single change it is behind, and for a permanent change the two are equivalent.
}

\DIFadd{The real-video results are more limited. On UCSD Ped2, CUHK Avenue and Mivia-IWDD, the per-video false-alarm rate is consistent with the nominal level on the two single-camera corpora, but their null footage is short (12.6\,s and 6.9\,min). On Mivia-IWDD, PatchCore raises alarms on 9 of 50 normal videos at $\alpha=0.1$, a point estimate above the nominal level whose interval contains it. The cause is not isolated: pooled calibration, serial dependence and boundary selection of the lag are all candidates. Alarm rates per hour of null footage are large, at 100.5 per hour for PatchCore on Mivia-IWDD. Power depends on the backbone and remains limited. Optical flow detects 11 of 12 }\DIFaddend Ped2 \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{, rather than on simulated streams alone. What it
finds is not unqualified success. The guarantee holds on both real corpora at
the lag each dataset's own diagnostic selects, and every classical baseline
tested fails to hold it on the same real streams --- but the window in which
validity and power coexist at all is narrow, narrower than any simulated
configuration in this paper, because real optical-flow scores are far more
autocorrelated than an AR(1) process and real anomalous episodes are
correspondingly brief.
Two directions followfrom that finding specifically. The nearer one }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{episodes but 3 of 74 Avenue episodes, and PatchCore detects 10 of 50 Mivia-IWDD dumping videos. Neither backbone is state of the art. On real video SENTINEL-E shows no demonstrated advantage over the baselines. Its claimed advantage, the horizon-free guarantee, is one that the short real videos cannot test.
}

\DIFadd{Three directions follow. The first }\DIFaddend is a stronger\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{backbone: what \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cref{sec:real-data} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
exposes is a
property of the scores, not of the sequential layer, and a backbonewhose
frame-to-frame errors are more nearly independent than optical-flow
magnitude's would widen the window without touching }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{, dumping-specific backbone, which would raise power without changing }\DIFaddend the theory. The \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{further
one is the corpus itself: this paper's real-data validation runs on pedestrian-walkway anomaly footage because no continuously-labelled real
illegal-dumping stream of the required length is publicly available, and
closing that gap needs a released corpus, from Mivia-IWDD or an operational
municipal deployment, rather than a change to the method. Neither the argument nor the algorithm is specific to dumpingin the first place.
Episodic, intermittent, recurring events observed through a
continuously-monitored sensor describe }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{second is per-camera calibration on Mivia-IWDD, which would test the guarantee under the condition it needs and show whether pooling explains the excess. The third is longer real streams, which would test the week-long simulation directly. Residue-specific change models would also separate the dumping action from the waste left behind. The argument and the algorithm do not depend on dumping, so the same optional-stopping problem applies to }\DIFaddend wildlife poaching, illegal fishing \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{,
gas leaks }\DIFdelend and infrastructure faults\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{as well as the loitering, wrong-direction
motion and abandoned-object anomalies the real corpora above already
represent; wherever a field evaluates on clips and deploys on streams, the
same optional-stopping problem and the same remedy apply}\DIFdelend .
